Many of Lumumba's supporters went east and formed the [Free Republic of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Republic_of_the_Congo) with its capital in [Stanleyville](588207.md) in opposition to Mobutu's government. Eventually, the government in Stanleyville agreed to rejoin with the Leopoldville government under the latter's rule,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoskyns1965375–377-157][^cite_note-LaFontaine16-158] however in 1963, Lumumba supporters formed another separate government in the east of the country and launched the [Simba rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simba_rebellion). The rebellion had support from the Soviet Union and many other countries in the Eastern Bloc.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVillafana201772–73-159] In November 1964, the U.S. and Belgium launched [Operation Dragon Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Dragon_Rouge) to rescue hostages taken by Simba rebels in Stanleyville. The operation was a success and expelled the Simba rebels from the city, leaving them in disarray. The Simbas were ultimately defeated the following year by the Congolese army.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartell201874–75-160][^cite_note-161]

After the March 1965 elections, Mobutu launched a [second coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Mobutu_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in November with the support of the U.S. and other powers. Mobutu Sese Seko claimed democracy would return in five years and he was popular initially.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENugent2004233-162] However, he instead took increasingly authoritarian powers eventually becoming the dictator of the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENugent2004233-162]

#### 1960: Laos

Main articles: [Laotian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laotian_Civil_War) and [1960 Laotian coups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Laotian_coups)

On August 9, 1960, Captain [Kong Le](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kong_Le) with his [Royal Lao Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Lao_Army) [paratroop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paratroop) battalion seized control of the administrative capital city of [Vientiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vientiane) in a bloodless coup on a "neutralist" platform with the stated aims of ending the [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laotian_Civil_War) raging in [Laos](17752.md), ending foreign interference in the country, ending the corruption caused by foreign aid, and better treatment for soldiers.[^cite_note-lcweb2.loc.gov-163][^cite_note-164] With CIA support, [Field Marshal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_Marshal) [Sarit Thanarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarit_Thanarat), the [Prime Minister of Thailand](273644.md), set up a covert [Royal Thai Armed Forces](30136.md) advisory group, called Kaw Taw. Kaw Taw together with the CIA backed a [November 1960 counter-coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Laotian_coups) against the new Neutralist government in Vientiane, supplying artillery, artillerymen, and advisers to General [Phoumi Nosavan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoumi_Nosavan), [first cousin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_cousin) of Sarit. It also deployed the [Police Aerial Reinforcement Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Patrol_Police#Border_Patrol_Police_Aerial_Reinforcement_Unit) (PARU) to operations within Laos, sponsored by the CIA.[^cite_note-165] With the help of CIA [front organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_organization) [Air America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_America_\(airline\)) to airlift war supplies and with other U.S. military assistance and covert aid from [Thailand](30128.md), General Phoumi Nosavan's forces captured Vientiane in November 1960.[^cite_note-lcweb2.loc.gov-163][^cite_note-166]

#### 1961: Dominican Republic

Main article: [Rafael Trujillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo)

In May 1961, the ruler of the [Dominican Republic](8060.md), [Rafael Trujillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo) was killed with weapons supplied by the United States [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) (CIA).[^cite_note-WHN-167][^cite_note-168] An internal CIA [memorandum](1050798.md) states that a 1973 [Office of Inspector General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_inspector_general_\(United_States\)) investigation into the assassination disclosed "quite extensive Agency involvement with the plotters." The CIA described its role in "changing" the government of the Dominican Republic as a 'success' in that it assisted in moving the Dominican Republic from a totalitarian dictatorship to a Western-style democracy."[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-Cord_Meyer-170] [Juan Bosch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Bosch_\(politician\)), an earlier recipient of CIA funding, was elected president of the Dominican Republic in 1962 and was deposed in 1963.[^cite_note-Diplomatic_History-171]

#### 1963: Iraq

Main article: [Ramadan Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan_Revolution)

Qasim in 1959

During the coup, the Ba'ath Party executed Iraq's prime minister, Brigadier [Abd al-Karim Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Karim_Qasim) (pictured), and desecrated his corpse on Iraqi television.

It has long been suspected that the Ba'ath Party collaborated with the CIA in planning and carrying out its violent coup that overthrew Iraq's leader, Brigadier [Abd al-Karim Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Karim_Qasim), on February 8, 1963.[^cite_note-172] Pertinent contemporary documents relating to the CIA's operations in Iraq have remained classified,[^cite_note-Osgood_p._16-124][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] and as of 2021 "\[s]cholars are only beginning to uncover the extent to which the United States was involved in organizing the coup",[^cite_note-175] but are "divided in their interpretations of American foreign policy."[^cite_note-Wolfe-Hunnicutt_2017-176] Bryan R. Gibson, writes that although "\[i]t is accepted among scholars that the CIA ... assisted the Ba'th Party in its overthrow of \[Qasim's] regime", that "barring the release of new information, the preponderance of evidence substantiates the conclusion that the CIA was not behind the February 1963 Ba'thist coup."[^cite_note-177]

Peter Hahn argues that "\[d]eclassified U.S. government documents offer no evidence to support" suggestions of direct U.S. involvement.[^cite_note-178] On the other hand, Brandon Wolfe-Hunnicutt cites "compelling evidence of an American role,"[^cite_note-Wolfe-Hunnicutt_2017-176] and that publicly declassified documents "largely substantiate the plausibility" of CIA involvement in the coup.[^cite_note-179] Eric Jacobsen, citing the testimony of contemporary prominent Ba'athists and U.S. government officials, states that "\[t]here is ample evidence that the CIA not only had contacts with the Iraqi Ba'th in the early sixties, but also assisted in the planning of the coup."[^cite_note-\:03-180] Nathan J. Citino writes that "Washington backed the movement by military officers linked to the pan-Arab Ba‘th Party that overthrew Qasim", but that "the extent of U.S. responsibility cannot be fully established on the basis of available documents", and that "\[a]lthough the United States did not initiate the 14 Ramadan coup, at best it condoned and at worst it contributed to the violence that followed."[^cite_note-181]

Ba'athist leaders maintained supportive relationships with U.S. officials before, during, and after the coup.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-\:12-183] A March 1964 State Department memorandum would state that U.S. "officers assiduously cultivated" a "Baathi student organization, which triggered the revolution of February 8, 1963 by sponsoring a successful student strike at the [University of Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Baghdad),"[^cite_note-184] and according to Wolfe-Hunnicutt, documents at the [Kennedy Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_Presidential_Library_and_Museum) suggest that the Kennedy administration viewed two prominent Ba'athist officials as "assets".[^cite_note-\:12-183]

Senior [National Security Council](44026.md) official [Robert Komer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Komer) wrote to President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) on February 8, 1963, that the Iraqi coup "is almost certainly a net gain for our side ... CIA had excellent reports on the plotting, but I doubt either they or UK should claim much credit for it."[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] The U.S. offered material support to the new Ba'athist government after the coup, amidst an anti-Communist purge and Iraqi atrocities against [Kurdish](17068.md) rebels and civilians,[^cite_note-187] and while it is unlikely that the Ba'athists would've needed assistance in identifying Iraqi Communists,[^cite_note-Batatu-188][^cite_note-\:2-189] it is widely believed that the CIA provided the Ba'athist National Guard with lists of Communists and other leftists, who were then arrested or killed.[^cite_note-190] Gibson emphasizes that the Ba'athists compiled their own lists, citing [Bureau of Intelligence and Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Intelligence_and_Research) reports.[^cite_note-191] On the other hand, Citino and Wolfe-Hunnicutt consider the assertions plausible because the [U.S. embassy in Iraq](4754335.md) had actually compiled such lists, were known to be in contact with the National Guard during the purge, and because National Guard members involved in the purge received training in the U.S.[^cite_note-\:2-189][^cite_note-192] Furthermore, Wolfe-Hunnicutt, citing contemporary U.S. counterinsurgency doctrine, notes that the assertions "would be consistent with American special warfare doctrine" regarding U.S. covert support to anti-Communist "Hunter-Killer" teams "seeking the violent overthrow of a communist dominated and supported government",[^cite_note-193] and draws parallels to other CIA operations in which lists of suspected Communists were compiled, such as [Guatemala in 1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Guatemalan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and [Indonesia in 1965–66](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_mass_killings_of_1965%E2%80%9366).[^cite_note-194]

#### 1964: British Guiana (Guyana)

According to author [John Prados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Prados), the CIA conducted a covert political campaign against left-wing Prime Minister of [British Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Guiana) [Cheddi Jagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheddi_Jagan) due to a fear of the spread of the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) across Latin America. The CIA supported trade unions during a strike against Jagan in 1963, and funded rival political parties during the 1964 election, while establishing the [Justice Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_Party_\(Guyana\)) as a CIA front. Political violence escalated, with nearly 200 murders and the bombing of Jagan's [People's Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Progressive_Party/Civic) (PPP) headquarters.[^cite_note-195]

#### 1964: Brazil

Main articles: [1964 Brazilian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Brazilian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), [Operation Brother Sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Brother_Sam), and [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor)



Since the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution), the [United States](3434750.md) started watching [Latin America](18524.md) to keep any socialist governments out, and in 1961, when the Brazilian president [Jânio Quadros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%A2nio_Quadros) resigned and the vice-president [João Goulart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Goulart) assumed power after the scandal of the [Legality Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_Campaign),[^cite_note-196] the United States started to get worried, as João Goulart had already shown sympathy for socialism, and slowly, [the relationship between Brazil and the United States](11633777.md) began deteriorating, with [Washington](108956.md) getting favorable on inciting a [coup d'état](25914154.md) to oust him. When João Goulart started talking about an [agrarian reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_reform), many groups, especially in the [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Military), started conspiring against him, with the idea of a coup d'état to overthrow him appearing and gaining force within the Brazilian population and military.

Political chaos ensued until the [March of the Family with God for Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_of_the_Family_with_God_for_Liberty) happened, of which many of those who opposed João Goulart went to the streets to protest against him.[^cite_note-197] When the [coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Brazilian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) broke out on March 31, 1964, the United States sent its [Navy](20518076.md) and [Air Force](32090.md) to help the military rebels through [Operation Brother Sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Brother_Sam). When the coup d'état ended up being successful and João Goulart was overthrown, a [right-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_dictatorship) [military dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Brazil) assumed power and ended up running the country until March 1985. The United States would also go on to support the Brazilian military dictatorship through [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor).[^cite_note-auto-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200]

#### 1965–1967: Indonesia

Main article: [Indonesian mass killings of 1965–66](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_mass_killings_of_1965%E2%80%9366)



Junior army officers and the commander of President [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno)'s palace guard accused senior [Indonesian National Armed Forces](14650.md) officers of planning a CIA-backed coup against Sukarno and killed six senior generals on October 1, 1965, in what came to be called the [30 September Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_September_Movement). The movement failed and subsequently the [Communist Party of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia) (PKI) was accused of planning the killing of the six generals,[^cite_note-201] as part of a propaganda campaign launched by the army. Civilian mobs were incited to attack those believed to be PKI supporters and other political opponents. Indonesian government forces with collaboration of some civilians perpetrated mass killings over many months. Scholars estimate the number of civilians killed range from a half million to over a million.[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203][^cite_note-indoholo-204] US Ambassador [Marshall Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Green) encouraged the military leaders to act forcefully against the political opponents.[^cite_note-205]

In 2017, declassified documents from the [US Embassy in Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Embassy_in_Jakarta) have confirmed that the US had knowledge of, facilitated and encouraged mass killings for its own geopolitical interests.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-Bevins2017-209] In 1990, US diplomats admitted to journalist [Kathy Kadane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathy_Kadane?action=edit\&redlink=1) that they had provided the Indonesian army with thousands of names of alleged PKI supporters and other alleged leftists, and that the U.S. officials then checked off from their lists those who had been killed.[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-210][^cite_note-211] President Sukarno's base of support was largely annihilated or imprisoned and the remainder terrified, enabling him to be forced out of power in 1967, replaced by an authoritarian military regime led by Suharto.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] Historian [John Roosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Roosa?action=edit\&redlink=1) states that "almost overnight the Indonesian government went from being a fierce voice for cold war neutrality and anti-imperialism to a quiet, compliant partner of the US world order."[^cite_note-Bevins2020-214]<sup>: 158 </sup> This campaign is considered a major turning point in the Cold War, and was such a success that it served as a model for other U.S.-backed coups and [anti-communist mass killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communist_mass_killings) campaigns throughout Asia and Latin America.[^cite_note-Bevins2017-209][^cite_note-215]

### 1970s

#### 1970–1979: Cambodia

Main articles: [Cambodian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_Civil_War), [1970 Cambodian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_Cambodian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), [Cambodian–Vietnamese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War), [Third Indochina War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Indochina_War), and [Allegations of United States support for the Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_United_States_support_for_the_Khmer_Rouge)



Prince [Norodom Sihanouk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norodom_Sihanouk), who came to power by the [1955 parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Cambodian_parliamentary_election), had for years kept the [Kingdom of Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia_\(1953%E2%80%931970\)) out of the [Vietnam War](32611.md) by being friendly with [China](5405.md) and [North Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam), and had integrated left wing parties into mainstream politics. A leftist uprising occurred in 1967 and the [Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Rouge) began an insurgency against the prince the following year.[^cite_note-216] Following the 1968 [Tet Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_Offensive), Sihanouk became convinced that North Vietnam was going to lose the war so he improved [relations with the United States](15982395.md). In March 1970, Sihanouk was deposed by right-wing General [Lon Nol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lon_Nol) following a vote of no confidence in Cambodia's National Assembly, and in October 1970, the [Khmer Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Republic) was declared by Lon Nol, officially ending the Kingdom and starting a period of military dictatorship. The overthrow followed Cambodia's constitutional process and most accounts emphasize the primacy of Cambodian actors in Sihanouk's removal. Historians are divided about the extent of U.S. involvement in or foreknowledge of the ouster, but an emerging consensus posits some culpability on the part of U.S. military intelligence.[^cite_note-217] There is evidence that "as early as late 1968" Lon Nol floated the idea of a coup to [U.S. military intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Intelligence_Agency) to obtain U.S. consent and military support for action against Prince Sihanouk and his government.[^cite_note-218]

The coup further destabilized the country and ushered in years of a [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_Civil_War) that from 1970 onwards, was being fought between Lon Nol's forces and the Khmer Rouge. Sihanouk created a [government in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_in_exile) called [GRUNK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GRUNK) which aligned itself with the Khmer Rouge to fight Lon Nol as a common enemy. To stop the Khmer Rouge from taking power in the country and also to disrupt North Vietnamese supply lines that passed through Cambodia, [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) and [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger) approved an intensified U.S. bombing in the countryside, in Operations [Menu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Menu) and [Freedom Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Freedom_Deal),[^cite_note-219] causing mass civilian loss which the Khmer Rouge used to promote recruitment and gain the support of the [CPK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Kampuchea) support.[^cite_note-220] Later, Kissinger suggested that Sihanouk had approved this U.S. bombing of North Vietnamese targets in Cambodia as early as 1969, although this has been heavily disputed by other sources.[^cite_note-221]

By 1973, the U.S. had already left [Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia) after seeing its objectives in Vietnam becoming increasingly harder, leaving the weakened Khmer Republic to collapse on April 17, 1975, when [Phnom Penh fell to the Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Phnom_Penh). After the fall of the Khmer Republic to the Khmer Rouge, the Khmer Rouge's leader [Pol Pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pol_Pot) was consolidated as the dictator of Cambodia, now renamed to [Kampuchea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Kampuchea). Because Sihanouk fought alongside the Khmer Rouge during the civil war, he was allowed to become Head of State, a ceremonial position,[^cite_note-222] however when he returned to the country and saw the [Cambodian genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_genocide) being perpetrated by the Khmer Rouge, he resigned. The Khmer Rouge did not accept this at first, but after some negotiation, they accepted,[^cite_note-223] after that, Sihanouk was placed under [house arrest](247643.md) until the [Third Indochina War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Indochina_War), when the [Angkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Kampuchea) permitted him to flee to China for safety.

Prince Norodom Sihanouk, for many the recognised leader of Cambodia, pictured with US President Ronald Reagan in the Oval Office, 11 October 1988.

[Norodom Sihanouk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norodom_Sihanouk) with [U.S. President](24113.md) [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) in the Oval Office in 1988. At the time, the [U.S.](3434750.md) still recognized the [CGDK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Government_of_Democratic_Kampuchea) as the legitimate government of Kampuchea.

There are many accusations of the United States supposedly supporting [Democratic Kampuchea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Kampuchea) during the [Cambodian–Vietnamese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War),[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] because Vietnam was supported by the Soviet Union, and the United States chose to support Vietnam's enemy, in this case Democratic Kampuchea. However, these claims are without support.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] Despite these responses, it is documented that the United States provided diplomatic support to the Khmer Rouge by continuously voting for Democratic Kampuchea and later the [CGDK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Government_of_Democratic_Kampuchea) to retain its seat at the [UN](31769.md), both immediately after its ousting as well as after it joined the coalition. This was because the Vietnamese-established [People's Republic of Kampuchea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Republic_of_Kampuchea) was a client state of Vietnam and more importantly, a [Soviet-aligned state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet-aligned_state).

#### 1970–1973: Chile

Main articles: [Project FUBELT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_FUBELT), [1973 Chilean coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Chilean_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), and [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor)



The U.S. government ran a [psy ops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_warfare) action in [Chile](5489.md) from 1963 until the coup d'état in 1973, and the CIA was involved in every [Chilean election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Chile) during that time. In the [1964 Chilean presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Chilean_presidential_election), the U.S. government supplied \$2.6 million in funding to [Christian Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_Party_\(Chile\)) presidential candidate [Eduardo Frei Montalva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Frei_Montalva), to prevent [Salvador Allende](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Allende) and the [Socialist Party of Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_Chile) winning. The U.S. also used the CIA to provide \$12 million in funding to business interests for use in harming Allende's reputation.[^cite_note-232]<sup>: 38–9 </sup>[Kristian C. Gustafson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristian_C._Gustafson?action=edit\&redlink=1) wrote:

> It was clear the Soviet Union was operating in Chile to ensure Marxist success, and from the contemporary American point of view, the United States was required to thwart this enemy influence: Soviet money and influence were clearly going into Chile to undermine its democracy, so U.S. funding would have to go into Chile to frustrate that pernicious influence.[^cite_note-Gust-233]

Prior to Allende's inauguration, chief of staff of the [Chilean Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilean_Army), [René Schneider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Schneider), a general dedicated to preserving the constitutional order and considered "a major stumbling block for military officers seeking to carry out a coup", was targeted in a failed CIA backed kidnapping attempt by General [Camilo Valenzuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camilo_Valenzuela) on October 19, 1970. Schneider was killed three days later in another botched kidnapping attempt led by General [Roberto Viaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Viaux).[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235] After the inauguration, there followed an extended period of social and political unrest between the right-dominated [Congress of Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Chile) and Allende, as well as [economic warfare](4757142.md) waged by [Washington](108956.md). U.S. President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) had promised to "make the economy scream" to "prevent Allende from coming to power or to unseat him".[^cite_note-Kornbluh-236] On September 11, 1973, President Allende was [overthrown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Chilean_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) by the [Chilean Armed Forces](5497.md) and [National Police](5446963.md), bringing to power the [regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_of_Chile_\(1973%E2%80%9390\)) of [Augusto Pinochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet). The CIA, through [Project FUBELT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_FUBELT) (also known as [Track II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_intervention_in_Chile#Track_II)), worked secretly to prepare the conditions for the coup. While the U.S. initially denied any involvement, many relevant documents have been declassified in the decades since.[^cite_note-Kornbluh-236]

According to historian Sebastián Hurtado Torres, there is no documentary evidence to support that the United States government acted actively in the coordination and execution of the [coup actions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Chilean_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) by the [Chilean Armed Forces](5497.md) from September 11, 1973 itself. However, Richard Nixon's interest from the beginning was that the Allende government would not be consolidated and intervened in the conditions prior to the Chilean coup of 1973.[^cite_note-Hurtado-237][^cite_note-1964-1973-238] Historian [Peter Winn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Winn) found "extensive evidence" of United States complicity in events that helped[^cite_note-p107-239] in part to create the conditions that led to the coup.[^cite_note-Winn_2010_270-271-240][^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] However, given the local political polarization and the performance of the Popular Unity government, there is no academic consensus on whether this intervention—which primarily took the form of financial support for the media, political parties, and opposition organizations—played a decisive role in the events of September 11, 1973.[^cite_note-1964-1973-238][^cite_note-La_Mano-243]

#### 1971: Bolivia

Main articles: [1971 Bolivian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Bolivian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), [Hugo Banzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Banzer), [Juan José Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Torres), and [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor)



The U.S. government supported the [1971 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Bolivian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) led by General [Hugo Banzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Banzer) that toppled President [Juan José Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Torres) of [Bolivia](3462.md), who had himself come to power in a coup the previous year.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] Torres was kidnapped and assassinated in 1976 as part of [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor).[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248]

#### 1974–1991: Ethiopia

See also: [Ethiopian Civil War](6572888.md)



Ethiopia pre-[Eritrean independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean_independence)

On September 12, 1974, [Emperor Haile Selassie I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haile_Selassie) of the [Ethiopian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Empire), a dynastic monarchy, was overthrown in a coup by the [Derg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derg), an organization set up by the Emperor to investigate the [Ethiopian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Armed_Forces).[^cite_note-249] The Derg, led by dictator [Mengistu Haile Mariam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mengistu_Haile_Mariam), became [Marxist–Leninist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism) and aligned with the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-guilty-250] Numerous rebel groups rose up against the Derg, including conservative, separatist groups, and other Marxist–Leninist groups.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] These groups would receive support from the United States.[^cite_note-254]

In the late 1980s, the rebels and the Eritrean separatists began to make gains against the government. The Derg dissolved itself in 1987, establishing the [People's Democratic Republic of Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Republic_of_Ethiopia) (PDRE) under the [Workers' Party of Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_of_Ethiopia) (WPE) in an attempt to maintain its rule. In 1990 the USSR stopped supporting the Ethiopian government as it started to collapse, while the United States continued to support the rebels.[^cite_note-\:02-255] In 1991 Mengistu Halie Mariam resigned and fled as rebels of the [Ethiopian People's Revolutionary Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_People's_Revolutionary_Democratic_Front) (EPRDF), a coalition of left-wing ethnic rebel groups, took over.[^cite_note-256] Despite the fact that the US opposed him, the US embassy helped Mariam escape to [Zimbabwe](34399.md).[^cite_note-257] The PDRE was dissolved and replaced with the [Tigray People's Liberation Front](1276759.md)-led [Transitional Government of Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transitional_Government_of_Ethiopia), and a transition to parliamentary democracy began.[^cite_note-dates-258]

#### 1975: Australia

This section is an excerpt from [Alleged CIA involvement in the Whitlam dismissal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal\&action=edit)]





Australian Prime Minister [Gough Whitlam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gough_Whitlam) (left) was dismissed by Governor-General [John Kerr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerr_\(governor-general\)) (right) amidst the [1975 Australian constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis). It has since been alleged the CIA was involved in the dismissal of Whitlam.

It is alleged that the [United States](3434750.md) [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) (CIA) was involved with the events leading up to the [1975 Australian constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_Australian_constitutional_crisis), in which [Prime Minister of Australia](24117.md) [Gough Whitlam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gough_Whitlam) was dismissed by [Governor-General of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia) [John Kerr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerr_\(governor-general\)), who had several ties to the CIA and its predecessor.[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:9-259][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:5-260][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:19-261][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:6-262][^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

CIA contractor and convicted Soviet spy [Christopher John Boyce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_John_Boyce), several authors, including [John Pilger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Pilger),[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_Pilger_coup-265][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:5-260][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:6-262] as well as some Australian politicians,[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:3-266] allege that the CIA backed Kerr to dismiss Whitlam, due to Whitlam's perceived left-wing policies[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:9-259] including Australian withdrawal from the [Vietnam War](32611.md), as well as his views on Australian sovereignty.[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:9-259] His conflict with the CIA is alleged[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:9-259] to have come to a head when he discovered several CIA-led operations occurring in Australia and overseas conducted by [ASIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Security_Intelligence_Organisation) and [ASIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Secret_Intelligence_Service), leading him to threaten cancellation of the lease on the [Pine Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine_Gap) facility, ending the US-led (nominally joint) operation, which was integral to the CIA's signals interception operations in the southern hemisphere.[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:10-267][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:5-260][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:9-259]

Whitlam's threat to not renew the lease on the Pine Gap facility was allegedly seen by the CIA as compromising the integrity of intelligence operations pertaining to the satellite projects [Rhyolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquacade_\(satellite\)) (subsequently Aquacade) and Argus (a proposed successor, later cancelled), used for monitoring and surveillance of missile launch sites in the Soviet Union and China,[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:1-268] which were unknown to the Australian government at the time despite a blanket sharing agreement between the two countries.[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_Pilger_coup-265]

Kerr denied any CIA involvement and Whitlam said Kerr did not need any encouragement from the CIA to sack him, and also denied his involvement in private communications, although Kerr allegedly maintained several links to CIA-funded organisations such as LawAsia and the magazine *[Quadrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrant_\(magazine\))*, and was referred to as "Our Man" by the CIA according to whistle-blower and leaker Christopher John Boyce.[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:2-269][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:3-266][^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:4-270]

The action of an unelected representative sacking an elected prime minister and replacing them with a caretaker prime minister was referred to by Australian Labor Party and former [Member of the House of Representatives](61565.md) [Peter Staples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Staples) as "the most blatant act of external interference in Australia's affairs and its autonomy as a nation and a democracy".[^cite_note-Alleged_CIA_involvement_in_the_Whitlam_dismissal_\:3-266]

#### 1975–1991: Angola

See also: [Angolan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angolan_Civil_War)

Beginning in the 1960s, a rebellion broke out against Portuguese colonial rule in the [Angolan War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angolan_War_of_Independence), mainly involving rebel groups the [People's Movement for the Liberation of Angola (MPLA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Movement_for_the_Liberation_of_Angola) and the [National Liberation Front of Angola (FNLA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Front_of_Angola). In 1974, the Portuguese regime of the [New State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\)) was depose in the [Carnation Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnation_Revolution). The new government promised to give independence to its colonies including Angola. On January 15, 1975, Portugal signed the [Alvor Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvor_Agreement) giving independence to Angola and establishing a transitional government including the MPLA, FNLA and [National Union for the Total Independence of Angola (UNITA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNITA). The transitional government consisted of the Portuguese High Commissioner, ruling with a Prime Ministerial Council (PMC) made up of three representatives, one from each Angolan party to the agreement, with a rotating premiership among the representatives; however, the various independence groups started fighting one another. The MPLA was a leftist group that was advancing upon the other two main rebel groups, the FNLA and UNITA, the latter led by [Jonas Savimbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonas_Savimbi), a former FNLA fighter and [Maoist](46636.md) who eventually became a capitalist ideologically and made UNITA into a capitalist militant group.[^cite_note-BBC25022002-271][^cite_note-272]

The United States covertly supported UNITA and the FNLA through [Operation IA Feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_IA_Feature). President [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) approved of the program on July 18, 1975, while receiving dissent from officials in the CIA and State Department. [Nathaniel Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Davis), [Assistant Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Assistant_Secretary_of_State), quit because of his disagreement with this.[^cite_note-davis-273][^cite_note-mulcahy-274] This program began as the war for independence was ending and continued as the civil war began in November 1975. The funding initially started at \$6 million but then added \$8 million on July 27 and added \$25 million in August.[^cite_note-start-275] The program was exposed and condemned by Congress in 1976. The [Clark Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Amendment) was added to the [US Arms Export Control Act of 1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_Export_Control_Act) ending the operation and restricting involvement in Angola.[^cite_note-termination-276] Despite this CIA Director [George H.W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) conceded that some aid to the FNLA and UNITA continued.[^cite_note-ns-277][^cite_note-con-278]



Location of Angola

In 1986, [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) articulated the [Reagan Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine), which called for the funding of anti-Communist forces across the world to "[roll back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollback)" Soviet influence. The [Reagan Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan) lobbied Congress to repeal the Clark Amendment, which eventually occurred on July 11, 1985.[^cite_note-clarkrepeal-279] In 1986, the war in Angola became a major Cold War proxy conflict. Savimbi's conservative allies in the US lobbied for increased support to UNITA.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] In 1986 Savimbi visited the White House and afterwards Reagan approved the shipment of [Stinger Surface-to-Air Missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIM-92_Stinger) as a part of \$25 million in aid.[^cite_note-linkage-282][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-crusade-284][^cite_note-demint-285]

After George H. W. Bush became president, aid to Savimbi continued. Savimbi began relying on the company [Black, Manafort, and Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black,_Manafort,_Stone_and_Kelly) in order to lobby for assistance. They lobbied the [H. W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_H._W._Bush) for increased assistance and weapons to UNITA.[^cite_note-286] Savimbi also met with Bush himself in 1990.[^cite_note-mobmeeting-287] In 1991, the MPLA and UNITA signed the [Bicesse Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicesse_Accords) ending US and Soviet involvement in the war, initiating multi-party elections and establishing the Republic of Angola, while South Africa withdrew from [Namibia](21292.md).[^cite_note-terms-288]

#### 1975–1999: East Timor

See also: [Indonesian invasion of East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_invasion_of_East_Timor)

On December 7, 1975, nine days after declaring independence from Portugal, [East Timor](19159399.md) was [invaded by Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_invasion_of_East_Timor).[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] Whilst it was under the pretext of [anti-colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-colonialism), the actual aim of the invasion was to overthrow the [Fretilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fretilin) regime [that emerged the previous year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnation_Revolution).[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] The day before the invasion, U.S. President [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) and Secretary of State, [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger) met with General [Suharto](217513.md),[^cite_note-293] who told them of his intention to invade East Timor. Ford replied, "\[W]e will understand and not press you on the issue. We understand the problem you have and the intentions you have."[^cite_note-Baldwin-294] Ford endorsed the invasion as he saw East Timor as of little significance, overshadowed by [Indonesia–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia%E2%80%93United_States_relations).[^cite_note-295] The [fall of Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Saigon) earlier in 1975 had left Indonesia as the most significant U.S. ally in [Southeast Asia](28741.md), so Ford reasoned that it was in the national interest to side with Indonesia.[^cite_note-296]

American weapons were crucial to Indonesia during the invasion,[^cite_note-297] with the majority of military equipment used by Indonesian military units involved being U.S. supplied.[^cite_note-298] [United States military aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_occupation_of_East_Timor#United_States) to Indonesia continued during its [occupation of East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_occupation_of_East_Timor), which ended in 1999 with East Timor's [independence referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_East_Timorese_independence_referendum).[^cite_note-299] In 2005, the final [Commission for Reception, Truth and Reconciliation in East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_for_Reception,_Truth_and_Reconciliation_in_East_Timor) wrote that "\[U.S.] political and military support were fundamental to the invasion and occupation of East Timor".[^cite_note-Baldwin-294][^cite_note-300]

#### 1976: Argentina

See also: [National Reorganization Process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Reorganization_Process), [Dirty War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirty_War), [1976 Argentine coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Argentine_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), and [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor)



[Jorge Rafael Videla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Rafael_Videla) meeting [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) in 1977

The [Argentine Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentine_Armed_Forces) overthrew [President](229473.md) [Isabel Perón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabel_Per%C3%B3n), elected in the [1973 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_1973_Argentine_presidential_election), in the [1976 Argentine coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Argentine_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), starting the military dictatorship of General [Jorge Rafael Videla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Rafael_Videla) known as the [National Reorganization Process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Reorganization_Process) until 1983. Both the coup and the following authoritarian regime were endorsed and supported by the U.S. government[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] with Henry Kissinger paying several official visits to Argentina during the dictatorship.[^cite_note-kissinger2-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-goni2-306] Videla and [Emilio Eduardo Massera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Eduardo_Massera) (a military officer and leading participant in the coup) were graduates of the [School of Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Hemisphere_Institute_for_Security_Cooperation#Graduates_of_the_School_of_the_Americas), which according to Congressman [Joseph P. Kennedy II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_P._Kennedy_II) was "...a school that has run more dictators than any other school in the history of the world."[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309]

#### 1979–1992: Afghanistan

Main articles: [CIA activities in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Afghanistan) and [Operation Cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone)



In 1978, the [Saur Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution) brought the [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan) to power, a one-party state backed by the Soviet Union. In what was known as [Operation Cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone), the U.S. government provided weapons and funding for a collection of warlords and several factions of [jihadi](16203.md) [guerrillas](12720.md) known as the [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) fighting to overthrow the Afghan government. The program began modestly with \$695,000 in nominally "non-lethal" aid to the mujahideen on July 3, 1979, and escalated following the December 1979 [Soviet invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-Coll-310][^cite_note-311]

Through the [Inter-Services Intelligence](2159021.md) (ISI) of neighboring [Pakistan](23235.md) the U.S. channeled training, weapons, and money for Afghan fighters.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] The first CIA-supplied weapons were antique British [Lee–Enfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee%E2%80%93Enfield) rifles shipped out in December 1979, but by September 1986 the program included U.S.-origin [state of the art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_the_art) weaponry, such as [FIM-92 Stinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIM-92_Stinger) [surface-to-air missiles](182664.md), some 2,300 of which were ultimately shipped into Afghanistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColl200458,_149–150,_337-316]

[Afghan Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Arabs) also "benefited indirectly from the CIA's funding, through the ISI and resistance organizations."[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] Some of the CIA's greatest Afghan beneficiaries were Islamist commanders such as [Jalaluddin Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalaluddin_Haqqani) and [Gulbuddin Hekmatyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulbuddin_Hekmatyar), who were key allies of [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) over many years.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] Some of the CIA-funded militants would become part of [al-Qaeda](1921.md) later on, and included bin Laden, according to former [Foreign Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Secretary_\(United_Kingdom\)) [Robin Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Cook) and other sources.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] Despite these and similar [allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_CIA_assistance_to_Osama_bin_Laden), there is no direct evidence of CIA contact with bin Laden or his inner circle during the Soviet–Afghan War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColl200487-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] The US also partially funded the radicalization of Afghan refugees in [Madrasas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa) like [Darul Uloom Haqqania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darul_Uloom_Haqqania) in Pakistan, whose alumni would later found groups like the [Taliban](30635.md).[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331]

U.S. support for the mujahideen ended in January 1992 pursuant to an agreement reached with the Soviets in September 1991 on ending external interference in Afghanistan by either side. By 1992, the combined U.S., Saudi, and Chinese aid to the mujahideen was estimated at \$6–12 billion, whereas Soviet military aid to Afghanistan was valued at \$36–48 billion. The result was a heavily armed, militarized Afghan society: Some sources indicate that Afghanistan was the world's top destination for personal weapons during the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColl2004232–233,_238-332]

### 1980s

#### 1980–1989: Poland

Main article: [Solidarity (Polish trade union)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\))



Since the [1952 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Polish_People's_Republic), [Poland](22936.md) was a one-party Communist state, the [Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People's_Republic). In the 1980s, opposition to it crystallised in the [Solidarity trade union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)), founded in 1980. The Reagan administration supported the Solidarity, and—based on CIA intelligence—waged a public relations campaign to deter what the Carter administration felt was "an imminent move by large Soviet military forces into Poland."[^cite_note-333] On November 4, 1982, President Reagan, after a brief discussion with the National Security Planning Group, signed an executive order to provide money and non-lethal aid to Polish opposition groups: the operation was code-named QRHELPFUL.[^cite_note-334] [Michael Reisman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Reisman?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [James E. Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_E._Baker) named operations in Poland as one of the covert actions of the CIA during [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-Arsanjani_2011_p.107-335] Colonel [Ryszard Kukliński](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryszard_Kukli%C5%84ski), a senior officer on the Polish General Staff was secretly sending reports to the CIA.[^cite_note-336]

The CIA transferred around \$2 million yearly in cash to Solidarity, for a total of \$10 million over five years. There were no direct links between the CIA and Solidarność, and all money was channeled through third parties.[^cite_note-337] CIA officers were barred from meeting Solidarity leaders, and the CIA's contacts with Solidarność activists were weaker than those of the [AFL–CIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFL%E2%80%93CIO), which raised \$300,000 from its members, which were used to provide material and cash directly to Solidarity, with no control of Solidarity's use of it. The U.S. Congress authorized the [National Endowment for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Endowment_for_Democracy) to promote democracy, and the NED allocated \$10 million to Solidarity.[^cite_note-338]

When the Polish government launched [martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Poland) in December 1981, however, Solidarity was not alerted. Potential explanations for this vary; some believe that the CIA was caught off guard, while others suggest that American policy-makers viewed an internal crackdown as preferable to an "inevitable Soviet intervention."[^cite_note-339] CIA support for Solidarity included money, equipment and training, which was coordinated by Special Operations.[^cite_note-340] [Henry Hyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Hyde), U.S. House intelligence committee member, stated that the US provided "supplies and technical assistance in terms of clandestine newspapers, broadcasting, propaganda, money, organizational help and advice".[^cite_note-341] Initial funds for covert actions by the CIA were \$2 million, but soon after authorization were increased and by 1985 the CIA successfully infiltrated Poland.[^cite_note-Executive_Secrets_page_201-203-342]

#### 1981–1982: Chad

See also: [CIA activities in Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Chad)



In 1975 as part of the [First Chadian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chadian_Civil_War_\(1965%E2%80%931979\)), the military overthrew [François Tombalbaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Tombalbaye) and installed [Félix Malloum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Malloum) as head of state. [Hissène Habré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiss%C3%A8ne_Habr%C3%A9) was appointed Prime minister, and attempted to overthrow the government in February 1979, failing, and being forced out. In 1979 Malloum resigned and [Goukouni Oueddei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goukouni_Oueddei) became head of state. Oueddei agreed to share power with Habré, appointing him Minister of Defense, but fighting resumed soon after. Habré was exiled to [Sudan](27421.md) in 1980.[^cite_note-343]

At the time the U.S. government wanted a bulwark against [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md) in [Libya](17633.md), and saw [Chad](5488.md), Libya's southern neighbor, as a good option. Chad and Libya had recently signed an agreement to attempt to end their [border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad%E2%80%93Libya_border) [conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chadian%E2%80%93Libyan_conflict) and "to work to achieve full unity between the two countries", which the United States was against. The United States also saw Oueddei as too close to Gaddafi. Habre was already pro-western and pro-American, as well as against Oueddei. The Reagan administration gave him covert support through the CIA when he returned in 1981 to continue fighting, and he overthrew Goukouni Oueddi on June 7, 1982, making himself the new president of Chad.[^cite_note-344] The CIA continued to support Habre after he took power, including training and equipping the Documentation and Security Directorate (DDS), Chad's notorious secret police. They also supported Chad in their [1986–1987 war against Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_War).[^cite_note-345]

#### 1981–1990: Nicaragua

See also: [CIA activities in Nicaragua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Nicaragua) and [Nicaraguan Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution)



In 1979, the [Sandinista National Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinista_National_Liberation_Front) (FSLN) overthrew the US-backed Somoza family. The [Carter administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jimmy_Carter) tried to be friendly with the new government, but the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan) that came after in January 1981 had more aggressive and anti-Communist foreign policy. They immediately cut off aid to the [Nicaraguan](21362.md) government, and in August 1981, Reagan signed National Security Decision Directive 7, which authorized the production and shipment of arms to the region but not their deployment. In November 1981 Reagan signed National Security Directive 17, which allowed covert support to anti-Sandinista forces.[^cite_note-justice.gov-346][^cite_note-347]

The U.S. government armed, trained and funded the [Contras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contras), a group of rebel fighters based in [Honduras](13394.md), in an attempt to overthrow the Nicaraguan government.[^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] As part of the training, the CIA distributed a detailed manual entitled "[Psychological Operations in Guerrilla War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_Operations_in_Guerrilla_War)", which instructed the Contras, among other things, on how to blow up public buildings, to assassinate judges, to create martyrs, and to blackmail ordinary citizens.[^cite_note-352] In addition to backing the Contras, the U.S. government also blew up bridges and [mined harbors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Nicaragua#Mining_of_Nicaraguan_harbors), causing the damaging of at least seven merchant ships and blowing up numerous Nicaraguan fishing boats. They also attacked [Corinto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corinto,_Nicaragua) harbor on October 10, 1983, causing 112 wounded according to the Nicaraguan government.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] After the [Boland Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boland_Amendment), passed in 1982, made it illegal for the U.S. government to provide funding for Contra activities, Reagan's administration secretly sold arms to the Iranian government to fund a secret U.S. government apparatus that continued illegally to fund the Contras, in what became known as the [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair).[^cite_note-358] The U.S. continued to arm and train the Contras even after the [Sandinista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinista) government of Nicaragua won the elections of 1984.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360]

In April 1984, Nicaragua [initiated proceedings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaragua_v._United_States) against the U.S. at the [International Court of Justice (ICJ)](14918.md) alleging that the U.S. violated international law for their support of the Contras and mining of harbors. Nicaragua argued that the U.S. had engaged in acts of aggression via surrogate forces to harm Nicaragua's economy and bring about regime change. The Nicaraguan government stated that economic cost of the war in 1984 alone was 255 million dollars and cited acts committed by the Contras such as closing of education and health care facilities, including a hospital, and the killing of health and education workers. The court ruled against the U.S. in June 1986 and awarded reparations to Nicaragua. However the Reagan administration had already announced in January 1985 that they would no longer be participating in the ICJ proceedings or consider itself bound by their ruling.[^cite_note-\:0-361][^cite_note-362]

During the [1990 Nicaraguan general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Nicaraguan_general_election) campaign, the [George H. W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_H._W._Bush) authorized 49.75 million dollars of non-lethal aid to the Contras. The war continued, with the Contras assassinating candidates and distributing leaflets promoting the opposition party UNO (National Opposition Union),[^cite_note-363] who would win the election.[^cite_note-364] The war ended soon afterwards.[^cite_note-365] In 1992, the post-Sandinista government of Nicaragua withdrew the complaint from the ICJ and repealed the law that had required the country to seek compensation from the U.S.[^cite_note-366]

#### 1983: Grenada

Main article: [United States invasion of Grenada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Grenada)



On October 25, 1983, the U.S. military and a coalition of six Caribbean nations [invaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Grenada) the nation of [Grenada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grenada), codenamed Operation Urgent Fury, and successfully overthrew the [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist) government of [Hudson Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Austin). The conflict was triggered by the killing of the previous leader of Grenada [Maurice Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Bishop) and the establishment of Hudson as the country's leader a week before on 19 October.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368] The [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) called the U.S. invasion "a flagrant violation of international law",[^cite_note-369] but a similar resolution widely supported in the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) was vetoed by the U.S.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371]

#### 1989–1994: Panama

Main article: [United States invasion of Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Panama)



In 1979, the U.S. and [Panama](22997.md) signed a [treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torrijos%E2%80%93Carter_Treaties) to end the [Panama Canal Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Canal_Zone) and promise that the U.S. would hand over the [canal](24849.md) after 1999. [Manuel Noriega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Noriega) ruled the country of Panama as a dictator. He was an ally of the United States working with them against the Sandinistas in Nicaragua and the FMLN in El Salvador. Despite this, relations began to deteriorate as he was implicated in the [Iran–Contra scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair), including drug trafficking.[^cite_note-National_Security_Archive-372] As relations continued to deteriorate Noriega started to ally with the Eastern Bloc. This also worried US officials and government officials like [Elliott Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elliott_Abrams) started arguing with Reagan that the US should invade Panama. Reagan decided to hold off due to [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush)'s ties to Noriega when he was the head of the CIA running his election, but after Bush was elected he started pressuring Noriega. Despite irregularities in the [1989 Panamanian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Panamanian_general_election), Noriega refused to allow the opposition candidate into power. Bush called on him to honor the will of the Panamanian people. Coup attempts were made against Noriega and skirmishes broke out between U.S. and Panamanian troops. Noriega was also indicted for drug charges in the United States.[^cite_note-jones-373] In December 1989, in a military operation code-named [Operation Just Cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Panama), the U.S. invaded Panama. Noriega went into hiding but was later captured by US forces. President-elect [Guillermo Endara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Endara) was sworn into office. The United States ended Operation Just Cause in January 1990 and began [Operation Promote Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Promote_Liberty), which was the occupation of the country to set up the new government until 1994.[^cite_note-usacac.army.mil-374]

#### 1986–1991: Soviet Union

Main articles: [Dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) and [Singing Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singing_Revolution)



In 1983, the congressionally funded [National Endowment for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Endowment_for_Democracy) was established to promote democratic change in Communist states.[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] Between 1984 and 1986, the foundation funded émigré journals that were smuggled into the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] At a meeting of the organization in December 1986, [Zbigniew Brzezinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zbigniew_Brzezinski) proposed supporting nationalism and democratic aspirations among national and religious minorities such as Ukrainians, Muslims, and the Baltics in order to politically and economically decentralize the Soviet system.[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] In 1989, sovietologist [Richard Pipes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Pipes) suggested that the Bush administration "devise a long-term strategy for the decolonization of the inner [Soviet empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_empire)", and Brzezinski argued that the Soviet Union should be transformed "into a genuinely voluntary confederation or commonwealth".[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] The foundation channeled aid to groups in the Baltic States, Armenia, Russia, and Ukraine that sought greater independence from [Gorbachev's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) central government.[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375]

Prior to the [1990 Russian parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Russian_Supreme_Soviet_election), NED funded an initiative by [Paul Weyrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Weyrich) and the [Free Congress Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Congress_Research_and_Education_Foundation) to assist [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) and a group of democratic candidates and to create a "communications network".[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] The foundation provided assistance to strengthen the independent press and to train democratic candidates in political techniques.[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] The organization [Democratic Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Russia) received \$2 million from the conservative [Krieble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_H._Krieble) Institute, with which Yeltsin's advisor [Gennady Burbulis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennady_Burbulis) organized 120 workshops and seminars in Moscow, democracy trainings in Russian regions, and conferences in Tallinn.[^cite_note-Zubok-376] The money also bought computers and copy machines that were used during the [referendum on March 17, 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_Union_referendum), as well as [Yeltsin's election campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin_1991_presidential_campaign).[^cite_note-Zubok-376] Yeltsin's [campaign manager in 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Russian_presidential_election), Alexander Urmanov, received training from the Krieble Institute.[^cite_note-Geoghegan-375] The KGB knew about the foreign aid, but did nothing about it because the recipients of the money had parliamentary immunity and there was no law prohibiting Soviet parliamentarians from receiving foreign aid.[^cite_note-Zubok-376] Commenting on Yeltsin's victory in Russia's first democratic presidential election, Burbublis told Krieble: "Well, Bob, you did it."[^cite_note-Zubok-376]

## 1991–present day

### 1990s

#### 1991: Iraq

See also: [1991 uprisings in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_uprisings_in_Iraq) and [Sanctions against Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iraq)



Iraq (orthographic projection)

The [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) (UNSC) imposed [sanctions against Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_against_Iraq) in August 1990 under [Resolution 661](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_661)[^cite_note-377] to compel Iraq to withdraw from [occupied Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait) without the use of military force, but Iraq refused to withdraw its forces, leading to the 1991 [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War).[^cite_note-378] During and immediately following the War, the United States broadcast signals encouraging an uprising against [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein), an autocrat who had ruled Iraq since coming to power in an internal struggle in the ruling [Ba'ath Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Socialist_Ba'ath_Party_%E2%80%93_Iraq_Region) in 1979.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] On February 24, 1991, a few days after the ceasefire was signed, the CIA-funded radio station [Voice of Free Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_Free_Iraq?action=edit\&redlink=1) called for the Iraqi people to rise up against Hussein.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-i440-382][^cite_note-f490-383]

The day after the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) ended on March 1, 1991, Bush again called for the overthrow of Saddam Hussein.[^cite_note-384] The U.S. was hoping for a coup but instead, a series of uprisings erupted across Iraq right after the war.[^cite_note-385] Two of the largest rebellions were led by the Iraqi Kurds in the North and the Shia militias in the south. Although George H.W. Bush said that the U.S. did not intend to assist any rebels,[^cite_note-386] the rebels assumed that they would get direct U.S. support; however, the United States worried that if Saddam fell and Iraq collapsed, Iran would gain power.[^cite_note-wounds-387] [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) wrote of his time as [Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff](364458.md) "our practical intention was to leave Baghdad enough power to survive as a threat to an Iran that remained bitterly hostile toward the United States".[^cite_note-inaction-388]

The Shia uprisings were crushed by the Iraqi military while the [Peshmerga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshmerga) were more successful, gaining the [Iraqi Kurds](35423251.md) autonomy. After the war, the U.S. government successfully advocated that sanctions remain in effect with revisions, including linkage to removal of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction), which the UNSC did in April 1991 by adopting [Resolution 687](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_687), albeit with the earlier prohibition on foodstuffs lifted.[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] U.S. officials stated in May 1991—when it was widely expected that the Iraqi government of Saddam Hussein faced collapse[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392]—that the sanctions would not be lifted unless Saddam was ousted.[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] In the subsequent president's administration, U.S. officials did not explicitly insist on regime change but took the position that the sanctions could be lifted if Iraq complied with all of the UN resolutions it was violating (including those related to the country's [human rights record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Ba'athist_Iraq)) and not just with UN weapons inspections.[^cite_note-396]

#### 1991: Haiti

Main article: [1991 Haitian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)

Eight months after his election, President [Jean-Bertrand Aristide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Bertrand_Aristide) was deposed by the [Haitian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Armed_Forces).[^cite_note-397] Many professors document that the CIA "paid key members of the coup regime forces, identified as drug traffickers, for information from the mid-1980s at least until the coup."[^cite_note-Whitney3202-398] Coup leaders [Raoul Cédras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_C%C3%A9dras) and [Michel François](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Fran%C3%A7ois) had received military training in the United States.[^cite_note-399] While CIA officials expressed displeasure with Aristide and CIA informants placed CIA officers with the military at the time of the coup, the CIA denied involvement.[^cite_note-400] Importantly, the U.S.-led [Operation Uphold Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uphold_Democracy) reinstated President Aristide after receiving approval for intervention by the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) and collaborating with other Caribbean nations.

#### 1992–1996: Iraq

The CIA launched DBACHILLES, a coup d'état operation against the Iraqi government, recruiting [Ayad Allawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayad_Allawi), who headed the [Iraqi National Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_National_Accord), a network of Iraqis who opposed the Saddam Hussein government, as part of the operation. The network included Iraqi military and intelligence officers but was penetrated by people loyal to the Iraqi government.[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] Also using Ayad Allawi and his network, the CIA directed a government [sabotage](29462.md) and bombing campaign in [Baghdad](4492.md) between 1992 and 1995.[^cite_note-Allawi-403] The CIA bombing campaign may have been merely a test of the operational capacity of the CIA's network of assets on the ground and not intended to be the launch of the coup strike itself.[^cite_note-Allawi-403] However, Allawi attempted a coup against Saddam Hussein in 1996. The coup was unsuccessful, but Ayad Allawi was later installed as prime minister of Iraq by the [Iraq Interim Governing Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Interim_Governing_Council), which had been created by the [U.S.-led coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-National_Force_%E2%80%93_Iraq) following the March 2003 invasion and occupation of Iraq.[^cite_note-404]

#### 1994–1995: Haiti

See also: [Operation Uphold Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uphold_Democracy)

After a right-wing military junta took over Haiti in 1991 in a coup, the U.S. initially had good relations with the new government. George H. W. Bush's administration supported the right wing junta. However, after the [1992 U.S. general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_elections) [Bill Clinton](3356.md) came to power. Clinton was supportive of returning [Jean-Bertrand Aristide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Bertrand_Aristide) to power, and his administration was active for the return of democracy to Haiti. This culminated in [United Nations Security Council Resolution 940](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_940), which authorized the United States to lead an invasion of Haiti and restore Aristide to power. A diplomatic effort was led by former U.S. president Jimmy Carter and former Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell).[^cite_note-405] The U.S. gave the Haitian government an ultimatum: either the dictator of Haiti, [Raoul Cédras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_C%C3%A9dras), retire peacefully and let Aristide come back to power, or be invaded and forced out. Cedras capitulated; however, he did not immediately disband the armed forces. Protesters fought the military and police.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] The U.S. sent in the military to stop the violence, and soon it was quelled. Aristide returned to lead the country in October 1994.[^cite_note-408] Clinton and Aristide presided over ceremonies and [Operation Uphold Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uphold_Democracy) officially ended on March 31, 1995.

#### 1996–1997: Zaire

See also: [First Congo War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Congo_War)

Due to the end of the Cold War, U.S. support for [Mobutu Sese Seko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobutu_Sese_Seko) in [Zaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaire) reduced.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410][^cite_note-Lemarchand,_René_2009._p._32-411] In 1990 the [Rwandan Patriotic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwandan_Patriotic_Front) (FPR) invaded Rwanda, beginning the [Rwandan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwandan_Civil_War), which culminated in the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md) and caused over 1.5 million refugees to flee into Zaire, where fighting broke out between refugee and non-refugee Tutsis, Hutu refugees, and other ethnic groups. In response, Rwanda formed Tutsi militias in Zaire,[^cite_note-reyn48-412] causing tensions between the militias and the Zaire government leading to the[^cite_note-413] Banyamulenge Rebellion on August 31, 1996, which led to the creation of Tutsi and non-Tutsi militias opposed to Mobutu into the [Alliance of Democratic Forces for the Liberation of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_of_Democratic_Forces_for_the_Liberation_of_the_Congo) (AFDL), led by [Laurent-Désiré Kabila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurent-D%C3%A9sir%C3%A9_Kabila).[^cite_note-ReferenceA-414]

The United States covertly supported Rwanda before and during the Congo war. The U.S. believed it was time for a "new generation of African leaders", such as Kagame and [Yoweri Museveni](293409.md) in Uganda, which was part of the reason the U.S. had previously stopped supporting Mobutu.[^cite_note-ken1-415] The U.S. sent soldiers to train the FPR and brought FPR commanders to the U.S. as well before the war in 1995 for training. During the war, rebels in Bukavu were joined by a group of African–American mercenaries, who claimed they had been recruited in an unofficial U.S. mission. The CIA and U.S. army set up communications in Uganda, and during the war, several aircraft landed in Kigali and Entebbe, claiming to be bringing "aid for the genocide victims"; however, it has been alleged that they were bringing military and communication supplies for the FPR. At the same time, U.S. operated anti-Mobutu support from the [International Rescue Committee](196452.md) (IRC).[^cite_note-416]

### 2000s

#### 2000: FR Yugoslavia

Main articles: [2000 Yugoslavian general election § Involvement of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Yugoslavian_general_election#Involvement_of_the_United_States), and [Overthrow of Slobodan Milošević § U.S. involvement in the revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87#U.S._involvement_in_the_revolution)



In the run-up to the [2000 Yugoslavian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Yugoslavian_general_election), the U.S. State Department actively supported opposition groups such as [Otpor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otpor) through the supply of promotional material and consulting services via Quangos.[^cite_note-417] United States involvement served to speed up and organize dissent through exposure, resources, moral and material encouragement, technological aid and professional advice.[^cite_note-Wilson346-418] This campaign was one of the factors contributing to the incumbent president's defeat in the 2000 Yugoslavian general election and subsequent [Bulldozer Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87) which overthrew Milošević on October 5, 2000, after he refused to recognise the results of the election.[^cite_note-Wilson346-418] In addition, President Bill Clinton authorized CIA involvement in the election to prevent Milošević's victory.[^cite_note-Shimer-419] The agency funneled "certainly millions of dollars" into the campaign against the Serbian leader domestically and also organized meetings of opposition members abroad.[^cite_note-Shimer-419]

#### 2001–2021: Afghanistan

Main articles: [United States invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan) and [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom)
